## Claude

# HLE — Module 10: Fault Tolerance, Self-Healing & Observability

**The interview questions:**
> "Your agent is calling a payer API that starts timing out. What happens?"
> "How do you know your agent's answer quality is degrading without a human checking?"
> "What does 'self-healing' mean for an AI agent?"

---

## Why This Is Different From Normal Fault Tolerance

Traditional API fault tolerance is simple: retry, return error code. AI agents are harder — three reasons:

```
Problem 1 — FAILURES ARE SILENT
  A normal API crash throws an exception. You see it immediately.
  An AI agent that gets bad RAG results doesn't crash.
  It produces a confident-sounding WRONG answer.
  The failure mode is hallucination, not an error.

Problem 2 — STATE IS MID-FLIGHT
  An agent 8 steps into a 12-step prior auth workflow that crashes
  has accumulated context that must be preserved — not thrown away.
  Restart from scratch = re-expose PHI, re-call APIs unnecessarily.

Problem 3 — CASCADING IS FAST
  Specialist agent returns bad data
  → Supervisor synthesizes a bad decision
  → Physician gets wrong clinical recommendation
  Three hops. Seconds elapsed. Patient safety at risk.
```

**Mental model:** ICU monitoring system — not just alarms when a patient flatlines, but when trends drift the wrong way hours before the crash.

---

## Layer 1 — Polly: Retry and Circuit Breaker

### Retry Policy

For transient failures — network blip, brief rate limit, temporary timeout.

```
payer API call fails
      ↓
Retry 1: wait 2s → try again
      ↓ still fails
Retry 2: wait 4s → try again
      ↓ still fails
Retry 3: wait 8s + random jitter → try again
      ↓ still fails
→ hand off to CircuitBreaker

WHY JITTER?
Without jitter: all 500 queued agents retry at exactly 2s, 4s, 8s
               → thundering herd → overwhelm the recovering API again
With jitter:   agents spread randomly across a time window
               → smooth load on recovery
```

```csharp
var retryPolicy = Policy
    .Handle<HttpRequestException>()
    .WaitAndRetryAsync(
        retryCount: 3,
        sleepDurationProvider: attempt =>
            TimeSpan.FromSeconds(Math.Pow(2, attempt))           // 2s, 4s, 8s
            + TimeSpan.FromMilliseconds(Random.Shared.Next(0, 500)) // jitter
    );
```

### Circuit Breaker

For sustained failures — the service is actually down, not just slow.

```
CLOSED (normal operation)
  ↓ 5 consecutive failures
OPEN (fail fast — no calls for 60 seconds)
  ↓ after 60 seconds
HALF-OPEN (send one probe call)
  ↓ probe succeeds
CLOSED (back to normal)
  ↓ probe fails
OPEN again (reset the 60s timer)
```

**Why circuit breaker matters for healthcare:**
```
BlueCross payer API goes down at 2pm.
500 prior auth requests are queued.

WITHOUT circuit breaker:
  Each request waits 30s timeout × 3 retries = 90s wasted per request
  500 × 90s = 12+ hours of wasted compute
  Physicians waiting. Patients delayed.

WITH circuit breaker:
  After 5 failures → OPEN
  All 500 requests fail IMMEDIATELY with CircuitOpenException
  Agent routes each one to pend queue with reason: "Policy API unavailable"
  Physicians see: "Pended — manual review in progress"
  When API recovers → circuit closes → replay from pend queue
```

---

## Layer 2 — Self-Healing (the system fixes itself)

### Pattern 1 — Groundedness Drift Detection

```
Every agent response gets a groundedness score (0.0 to 1.0)
You track the rolling average over a time window

Normal operation:   avg groundedness = 0.93
After prompt update: avg groundedness = 0.81 ← drift detected!

System detects drift below threshold (0.85 for JM Family, 0.90 for VitalCare)
→ fires alert to engineering team
→ triggers automatic prompt rollback (see Pattern 2)

Why it matters: a prompt update might have introduced subtle drift.
Without this: nobody notices for days. Physicians get degraded answers.
With this:    caught within minutes.
```

### Pattern 2 — Automatic Prompt Rollback

```
Groundedness drops below threshold
      ↓
System automatically:
  1. Identifies the last known-good prompt version in Git
  2. Reverts prompt to that version
  3. Redeploys to the agent
  4. Fires Teams alert: "Groundedness drift detected — rolled back to v1.1.0"
  5. Logs incident: before score, after score, which prompt version

This is LLMOps in action — same concept as blue-green deployment rollback
applied to AI prompts instead of code binaries.
```

### Pattern 3 — Agent Loop Guard

```
Agent can enter a failure loop:
call tool → tool fails → retry → still fails → retry infinitely

Guard in Semantic Kernel:
if (toolCallCount > MAX_TOOL_CALLS)  // 8 for VitalCare, 10 for JM Family
{
    cancel the loop
    escalate to human review queue
    attach full session trace
}

Physician sees: "Agent could not complete — routed to reviewer"
Not: silent hang, not: infinite retry burning API quota
```

### Pattern 4 — Dead-Letter Replay

```
Message fails to deliver (agent crashes, specialist unavailable)
      ↓
Message goes to DEAD-LETTER QUEUE (Azure Service Bus)
  └── Full envelope preserved: MessageId, CorrelationId, payload
  └── Alert fires to on-call engineer
  └── Workflow: PENDED (not abandoned)

Root cause fixed (API recovered, agent redeployed):
      ↓
Replay worker reads dead-letter
→ re-publishes to main queue
→ workflow resumes

No human re-submission needed.
No PHI data lost.
```

---

## Layer 3 — Observability: Three Layers

This is what separates AI architects from developers. Standard monitoring watches if the service is up. AI monitoring watches if the service is **right**.

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  LAYER 1: INFRASTRUCTURE                                │
│  Is the compute healthy?                                │
│                                                         │
│  CPU: 42%   Memory: 61%   Pod restarts: 0              │
│  Queue depth: 12 messages (backlog growing? = problem)  │
│                                                         │
│  Tool: Azure Monitor + Container Insights               │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  LAYER 2: AI SERVICE                                    │
│  Is the LLM responding correctly?                       │
│                                                         │
│  Tokens/request: 1,847   Latency p95: 2.3s             │
│  Rate limit hits (429s): 0   Cost/hr: $4.20            │
│  TPM utilization: 68%                                   │
│                                                         │
│  Tool: Azure OpenAI metrics + App Insights custom events│
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  LAYER 3: QUALITY                                       │
│  Is the agent answering CORRECTLY?                      │
│                                                         │
│  Groundedness: 0.91 ✅   Relevance: 0.88 ✅            │
│  Rolling 1hr avg: 0.89 (threshold: 0.85) ✅            │
│  Hallucination rate: 2.1% ✅                            │
│                                                         │
│  Tool: Azure AI Foundry Evaluation + App Insights       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**The rule:** Layer 1 alerts when the service crashes. Any engineer builds Layer 1. An AI architect builds all three. Layer 3 is what standard monitoring completely misses — perfect uptime, hallucinating answers.

---

## CV Skill: LLMOps — Complete Practice

> "How do you manage prompts and model deployments in production?"

### Prompt Versioning in Git

```
prompts/
├── clinical_pa_agent/
│   ├── v1.0.0.txt    ← original
│   ├── v1.1.0.txt    ← added few-shot examples
│   └── v2.0.0.txt    ← new output schema (breaking change)
└── ambient_doc_agent/
    └── v1.0.0.txt

Every change goes through PR.
Clinical + legal review required before merge to main.
Production = main branch. Simple as that.

Debugging regressions:
"Groundedness dropped on 2026-06-22"
→ git log shows prompt v2.0 merged 2026-06-21
→ root cause identified in minutes, not days
```

### Automated Evaluation Pipeline (Azure DevOps)

```
Trigger: any change to prompts/ folder

Stage 1 — LINT
└── Validate: max token count, required sections present,
              security instructions present, output schema valid

Stage 2 — GOLDEN DATASET EVALUATION
└── Run new prompt against 100 curated test cases in Azure AI Foundry
└── Score: groundedness, relevance, coherence
└── Generate comparison: new prompt vs current production

Stage 3 — QUALITY GATE
├── Groundedness ≥ 0.90  ✅ → pass
├── Relevance ≥ 0.85     ✅ → pass
└── No regression on 15 critical cases ✅ → pass
    Any gate fails → PR BLOCKED, team notified, no merge

Stage 4 — SHADOW DEPLOYMENT
└── New prompt gets 10% of live traffic
└── Monitor for 24 hours
└── Metrics hold → promote to 100%
└── Metrics degrade → auto-rollback to previous
```

### Model Deployment — Blue-Green Traffic Shifting

```
Production today:
├── Active:  GPT-4o 2025-04-01 → 90% traffic
└── Canary:  GPT-4o 2025-06-01 → 10% traffic

Promotion steps:
10% → watch quality metrics 1hr → ok
50% → watch quality metrics 1hr → ok
100% → fully promoted

Quality degrades at any step → rollback to previous 100%
Single pipeline trigger. No manual intervention.
```

---

## JM Family Anchor

> "At JM Family, every external call — Document Intelligence, Azure AI Search, Azure OpenAI — goes through a Polly RetryPolicy with 3 retries, exponential backoff and jitter, and a CircuitBreaker that opens after 5 consecutive failures and stays open for 60 seconds. App Insights captures token usage, latency, and cost per request. Groundedness evaluation runs on a 20-sample rolling window every 30 minutes — if it drops below 0.85 we get a Teams alert. We don't yet have automatic prompt rollback in production — that's our next LLMOps maturity step. For VitalCare I'd implement the full pipeline: Git-versioned prompts, automated evaluation gate, canary deployment, and auto-rollback on drift."

---

## Interview Answers

**"Your agent is calling a payer API that starts timing out. What happens?"**
> "Two patterns work together. Polly RetryPolicy handles transient failures — 3 retries with exponential backoff and jitter to prevent thundering herd on recovery. If it's still failing after 3 retries, the CircuitBreaker takes over — after 5 consecutive failures the circuit opens and all calls fail immediately instead of waiting for timeout. That converts a 90-second timeout pile-up into instant failures. Agents route failed requests to a pend queue with the reason attached. When the circuit closes on recovery, a replay worker re-processes from the dead-letter queue automatically."

**"How do you know your agent's quality is degrading without a human checking?"**
> "Three-layer monitoring. Infrastructure layer is standard — CPU, memory, queue depth. AI service layer watches token usage, latency percentiles, rate limit hits. Quality layer is the one that catches silent degradation — I track groundedness scores on a rolling sample window every 30 minutes. If the rolling average drops below threshold, the system fires an alert and triggers automatic prompt rollback. A perfect uptime score means nothing if the agent is hallucinating clinical decisions. Layer 3 is what catches that."

**"What does self-healing mean for an AI agent?"**
> "Three things. First, groundedness drift detection triggers automatic prompt rollback when answer quality degrades — the system returns to the last known-good prompt without human intervention. Second, circuit breaker auto-recovery — when the downstream service comes back, the circuit transitions half-open, sends a probe, and closes automatically. Third, dead-letter replay — once the root cause is fixed, failed messages are replayed from the queue without human resubmission. The goal is: recoverable failures self-resolve, unrecoverable failures route to human escalation with a complete audit trail."

---

Module 10 done. The three mental models to hold: **Polly handles transient failures, circuit breaker handles sustained failures, groundedness monitoring catches silent quality failures**. Standard monitoring misses that third one — that's your differentiator.

## The Most Important Mental Model for This Module

```
WRONG framing: "Let me explain what I wrote in the assessment"

RIGHT framing: "I built this at JM Family, here's what I changed for healthcare"

The interviewer wants to hear PRODUCTION EXPERIENCE, not a document walkthrough.
Every answer starts at JM Family, then explains the VitalCare delta.
```

## The JMA → VitalCare Bridge

This is the map you use to anchor every answer:

```
JM FAMILY                          VITALCARE                    HEALTHCARE DELTA
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Dealer form extraction (DI)   →   Prior auth form (DI)          Confidence: 0.85 → 0.95
                                                                 PHI consequence is higher

                                                                 Clinical specificity matters

                                                                 No JMA equivalent

                                                                 PHI retention minimization

                                                                 Never name/DOB/diagnosis

Escalation → Sales Manager    →   Escalation → Pharmacist       Requires clinical license

                                                                 HIPAA minimum necessary
```

## The 5 Decisions You Must Defend Cold

These are what the interviewer will probe most.

---

### Decision 1 — Azure DI over John Snow Labs for form extraction

```
Why they'll ask: "Your assessment uses Azure DI for all document processing.
                  But JSL is the gold standard for clinical NLP. Why didn't you use it?"

The answer:
"Prior auth FORMS are structured — fixed field positions, checkboxes,
 signature lines, printed labels. Azure DI Custom Template handles this
 with >95% accuracy after training on 10-15 labeled samples per form type.

 John Snow Labs wins on clinical NARRATIVE — SOAP notes, discharge summaries —
 where medical NER, RxNorm normalization, and ICD-10 extraction matter.
 The incoming prior auth form is structured, not narrative.
 DI is the right tool for that specific job.

 If we add ambient documentation or discharge planning to the pipeline,
 JSL enters at that stage — that's where narrative lives."

One sentence: Form structure → Azure DI. Medical meaning → John Snow Labs.
```

---

### Decision 2 — Semantic Kernel over LangGraph

```
Why they'll ask: "LangGraph has better crash recovery and human-in-the-loop.
                  Why Semantic Kernel for a complex clinical workflow?"

The answer:
"The platform is Azure-native, team is predominantly .NET.
 SK gives Managed Identity, Content Safety, Azure AI Search as first-class connectors.
 LangGraph is Python-only — splits the team and removes Azure-native auth.

 BUT — I'd bring LangGraph in for one specific sub-problem:
 the step therapy decision tree.
 It has 7 conditional branches and physician interrupt gates at two points.
 LangGraph's Checkpointer + interrupt_before is cleaner than manual state in SK.

 So: SK as the main orchestration layer.
     LangGraph isolated for that one graph-shaped sub-workflow."

One sentence: SK for the platform. LangGraph for complex branching sub-workflows.
```

---

### Decision 3 — RAG over fine-tuning for the clinical knowledge base

```
Why they'll ask: "Fine-tuning would make the model more accurate on clinical data.
                  Why did you choose RAG?"

Three reasons — say all three:

1. KNOWLEDGE CHANGES
   "Clinical policies change weekly — formulary updates, payer rule changes,
    new treatment guidelines. Fine-tuning freezes knowledge at training time.
    RAG retrieves the current document on every call."

2. AUDITABILITY
   "In a HIPAA environment, every decision needs a paper trail.
    RAG can cite: 'Answer sourced from BlueCross formulary v3.2, section 7.4.'
    Fine-tuning cannot. 'The model learned it during training' is not defensible."

3. COST AND SPEED
   "Fine-tuning GPT-4o for this domain costs tens of thousands of dollars
    and takes weeks. RAG with Azure AI Search is production-ready in days.
    And when a payer changes their formulary, RAG updates by re-indexing a PDF.
    Fine-tuning requires a full retraining cycle."
```

---

### Decision 4 — Hybrid MCP + APIM over direct API calls

```
Why they'll ask: "That's two layers of infrastructure. Why not just call the payer APIs directly?"

The scale argument:
"180 hospitals. 12 agent types. Each hospital potentially on different EHR versions.
 Direct API calls = 180 × 12 = 2,160 custom integrations to maintain.

 MCP Hub provides tool discovery — each agent asks 'what tools do I have?'
 Hub returns current schemas. When Hospital X upgrades Epic version,
 we update ONE MCP connector, not 12 agents.

 APIM sits in front of backends for:
 ├── HIPAA audit logging (every payer API call logged)
 ├── Rate limiting (runaway agent can't exhaust API quotas)
 └── Centralized OAuth (one place to rotate credentials)

 The combination is the only pattern that scales to 180 hospitals."
```

---

### Decision 5 — Supervisor + Specialist hierarchy over one monolithic agent

```
Why they'll ask: "One agent with all the tools is simpler. Why the overhead of a hierarchy?"

"A monolithic prior auth agent would have a 2,000-token system prompt
 trying to be an eligibility checker, formulary expert, and fraud detector.
 The LLM's attention is diluted. Tools from all three domains compete.

 Specialist pattern:
 ├── Each specialist: 200-token focused system prompt, 3-5 tools max
 ├── Accuracy improves — model focuses on exactly one domain
 ├── Maintainability improves — change fraud rules = touch only FraudDetector
 ├── Specialists run IN PARALLEL
 │   Result arrives in time of SLOWEST specialist
 │   Not: sum of all three (2s vs 6s for three 2s specialists)
 └── Failure isolated — FraudDetector down ≠ entire workflow down"
```

---

## The Two VitalCare Terror Questions

### "GPU costs tripled. CTO is questioning ROI. Defend your cost model."

```
"We use Azure OpenAI serverless — no GPU provisioning, no idle costs.
 Three cost controls:

 1. Model tier routing
    GPT-4o mini → classification, routing, duplicate detection (17x cheaper)
    GPT-4o      → final synthesis only (reserved for complex reasoning)

 2. Semantic caching via Redis
    Same prior auth + same policy answered in last 24hrs → return cached response
    No LLM call. Cache hit rate: 25-30% for formulary questions.

 3. Top-K = 3 in RAG
    3 context chunks per prompt, not 10.
    Fewer tokens → lower cost per call.

 The ROI case:
 180 hospitals × 200 prior auths/day × 12 minutes saved per auth
 = 432,000 minutes/day = 7,200 clinician-hours/day
 At $80/hour clinical staff cost → $576,000/day in recovered clinical time."
```

### "A physician claims your agent gave a wrong recommendation that delayed a patient. How do you investigate?"

```
"Every decision has a CorrelationId that links the full audit trail.

 Step 1: Pull the audit log for that CorrelationId from App Insights
         → see every agent call, tool result, confidence score

 Step 2: Check the groundedness score
         If < 0.90 → process gap (should have been pended, not approved)

 Step 3: Check which formulary version was retrieved
         Outdated policy document → retrieval index issue

 Step 4: Check Content Safety filter log
         Any suppressed output that changed the recommendation?

 Investigation time: under 10 minutes.
 Because every decision is fully logged at the time it happens.

 Root cause actions:
 ├── Outdated policy → re-index updated formulary PDF
 ├── Low groundedness → lower confidence threshold → more pends
 └── Specific insurer issue → mandatory pharmacist review for that insurer"
```

---

## PHI Rules You Must Know Cold

```
What goes IN THE LOGS:
✅ Member ID (anonymized identifier)
✅ CorrelationId
✅ Agent name
✅ Tool called
✅ Confidence score
✅ Timestamp

What NEVER goes in logs:
❌ Patient name
❌ Date of birth
❌ Diagnosis code
❌ Medication name
❌ Any of the 18 HIPAA identifiers

FunctionInvocationFilter enforces this automatically.
If an engineer adds a console.log(patientRecord) during debugging?
The filter intercepts before it reaches App Insights.

The 7-year HIPAA rule:
Audit logs → Cosmos DB with append-only policy + legal hold
Nobody can delete a log entry — not even admins.
```

---

## The Single Most Important Answer

**"Walk me through the key decisions in your assessment."**

Say this — five decisions, 90 seconds:

> "Five load-bearing decisions. RAG over fine-tuning — clinical policies change weekly, RAG stays current and is auditable. Azure DI for structured form extraction — Custom Template handles fixed-layout prior auth forms at 95% accuracy without JSL's NLP overhead. Semantic Kernel for orchestration — .NET-native, Azure-integrated, Managed Identity throughout. Hybrid MCP + APIM — scales to 180 hospitals by updating one MCP connector per hospital upgrade, not 12 agents. Supervisor plus specialist hierarchy — parallel execution, focused system prompts, clean failure isolation. All five decisions connect back to clinical safety, PHI compliance, or operational scale."

## Claude

Great practical question. The honest answer is: **mix of both, but most needs custom code**.

---

## Stage 1 — Pre-Processing (de-noise, de-skew, binarize)

**100% custom code. Azure does nothing here automatically.**

```
Azure Document Intelligence receives your image and runs OCR.
It does NOT clean the image first.

YOU write an Azure Function that:
├── Takes the raw scan from Blob Storage
├── Runs OpenCV (Python) or ImageSharp (C#) to:
│   ├── de-noise  → median filter
│   ├── de-skew   → Hough transform + rotate
│   └── binarize  → adaptive threshold
└── Sends the CLEANED image to Azure DI

If you skip this stage and send the raw scan directly to DI:
└── Confidence scores suffer (JM Family: 0.78 before de-skew)
```

No portal config here — this is code you write and deploy.

---

## Stage 2 — OCR Engine (Azure Document Intelligence)

**Mix: portal configuration + SDK code.**

```
CONFIGURATION (portal — no code):
├── Choose model type in Document Intelligence Studio
│   ├── Prebuilt → just select it, ready immediately
│   ├── Custom Template → upload 5-15 labeled samples,
│   │   draw field boundaries in the UI, click Train
│   └── Custom Neural → same labeling UI, more samples needed
└── Training happens in the cloud — you click a button

SDK CODE (your Azure Function):
var operation = await _client.StartAnalyzeDocumentAsync(
    "your-custom-model-id", documentStream);
var result = await operation.WaitForCompletionAsync();

// Then read result.Documents[0].Fields
// Each field has: Content (value) + Confidence (score)
```

**Summary:**
- Training the model = portal configuration
- Calling the model = SDK code (few lines)
- Reading results + confidence routing = your code

---

## Stage 3 — Post-Processing (format, consistency, business rules)

**100% custom code. Nothing provided out of the box.**

```
Azure DI gives you extracted field values.
It does NOT validate them. That's your job.

You write:
├── Format validation
│   └── if (!DateTime.TryParse(extractedDate)) → flag for review

├── Cross-field consistency
│   └── if (dischargeDate < admissionDate) → flag for review

└── Business rules
    └── if (!policyNumber.StartsWith("BCB-")) → flag for review

Then confidence routing:
└── > 0.90 → auto-process
└── 0.70–0.90 → human review queue
└── < 0.70 → dead-letter queue
```

---

## Summary Table

| Stage | Custom Code | Configuration |
|---|---|---|
| Pre-processing (de-noise, de-skew, binarize) | ✅ Yes — OpenCV/ImageSharp | ❌ None |
| OCR Engine — model training | ❌ None | ✅ Yes — Document Intelligence Studio |
| OCR Engine — API call | ✅ Yes — SDK call (few lines) | ❌ None |
| Post-processing (validation + routing) | ✅ Yes — your validation logic | ❌ None |

**The only part that's truly "configuration with no code" is labeling and training the Custom Template model in the portal.** Everything else — before it and after it — is code you write.

## You

whats opecv and image sharp does it needs any sdk ?

## Claude

Yes, both are libraries you install via NuGet / pip. Here's the plain explanation:

---

## OpenCV

**What it is:** Open-source computer vision library. The industry standard for image processing — used everywhere from cameras to robotics to AI pipelines.

**Language:** Python (most common for AI pipelines) or C++

**How to install:**
```bash
pip install opencv-python
```

**What it does in your OCR pipeline:**
```python
import cv2
import numpy as np

# Load image
img = cv2.imread("patient_form.jpg")

# De-noise (median filter)
denoised = cv2.medianBlur(img, 3)

# Convert to grayscale (prep for binarize)
gray = cv2.cvtColor(denoised, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)

# De-skew (detect angle + rotate)
coords = np.column_stack(np.where(gray > 0))
angle = cv2.minAreaRect(coords)[-1]
rotated = cv2.rotate(gray, angle)

# Binarize (adaptive threshold)
binary = cv2.adaptiveThreshold(
    rotated, 255,
    cv2.ADAPTIVE_THRESH_GAUSSIAN_C,
    cv2.THRESH_BINARY, 11, 2
)

# Send binary image to Azure Document Intelligence
```

---

## ImageSharp

**What it is:** The .NET equivalent of OpenCV — a C# image processing library. You use this if your pipeline is C#.

**Language:** C# / .NET

**How to install:**
```bash
dotnet add package SixLabors.ImageSharp
```

**What it does — same job, different language:**
```csharp
using SixLabors.ImageSharp;
using SixLabors.ImageSharp.Processing;

using var image = Image.Load("patient_form.jpg");

image.Mutate(x => x
    .GaussianBlur(0.5f)      // de-noise
    .Grayscale()              // prep for binarize
    .BinaryThreshold(0.5f)   // binarize
);

image.Save("cleaned_form.jpg");
// Send cleaned image to Azure DI
```

---

## Which One Do You Use?

```
Your pipeline is Python?  →  OpenCV (pip install opencv-python)
Your pipeline is C#/.NET? →  ImageSharp (NuGet: SixLabors.ImageSharp)

At JM Family (.NET shop):
└── ImageSharp for any C# preprocessing
└── OR skip custom preprocessing and let Azure DI handle it
    (DI has basic built-in preprocessing for clean scans)

For VitalCare (healthcare, high accuracy needed):
└── Custom preprocessing BEFORE DI is worth it
└── JM Family proof: de-skew alone moved confidence 0.78 → 0.91
```

---

## The Quick Answer for the Interview

> "Pre-processing is a custom Azure Function — if the team is .NET we use ImageSharp, if Python we use OpenCV. Both are open-source libraries installed via NuGet or pip. We clean the image first — de-noise, de-skew, binarize — then pass the cleaned image to Azure Document Intelligence. The library itself is free; the compute cost is the Azure Function execution time, which is negligible compared to the DI API call cost."

## The Accurate Picture

**Azure DI has SOME basic built-in preprocessing — I was wrong to say "Azure does nothing."**

```
What Azure DI handles internally (automatically):
├── Minor rotation (1-2 degrees)
├── Basic noise reduction on clean scans
└── PDF page rendering

What Azure DI does NOT handle well:
├── Significant skew (3+ degrees) → accuracy drops
├── Heavy scan noise (fax artifacts, speckle) → misreads characters
└── Uneven lighting / dark corners → binarization fails
```

---

## The Correct Rule

```
Document quality is GOOD (clean office scan, digital PDF)?
└── Skip custom preprocessing
└── Send directly to Azure DI
└── DI's built-in handling is sufficient

Document quality is POOR (faxed, aged paper, heavy tilt, speckle)?
└── Add custom preprocessing (OpenCV / ImageSharp) BEFORE DI
└── This is the JM Family case:
    └── Dealer forms arrived tilted, faxed, with artifacts
    └── DI alone: confidence 0.78 (not enough)
    └── After custom de-skew: confidence 0.91
```

---

## Corrected Statement

~~"100% custom code. Azure does nothing here automatically."~~

**Correct:** Azure DI handles basic preprocessing automatically. You add custom preprocessing (OpenCV / ImageSharp) **only when** document quality is poor enough that DI's built-in is not sufficient — which in healthcare is common because of faxes, aging documents, and poor scanners.

## Claude

SDK calls — not heavy code at all. Those scary terms (Hough transform, adaptive threshold) are just **function names inside the OpenCV/ImageSharp library**.

You call them in 2-3 lines each:

```python
import cv2

img = cv2.imread("form.jpg")

# De-noise → one function call
denoised = cv2.medianBlur(img, 3)

# De-skew → two function calls
angle = cv2.minAreaRect(np.column_stack(np.where(img > 0)))[-1]
straight = cv2.rotate(denoised, angle)

# Binarize → one function call
binary = cv2.adaptiveThreshold(straight, 255,
             cv2.ADAPTIVE_THRESH_GAUSSIAN_C, cv2.THRESH_BINARY, 11, 2)
```

---

## The Reality

```
You do NOT write the median filter algorithm.
You do NOT write the Hough transform math.
You do NOT write the adaptive threshold logic.

OpenCV already wrote all of that.
You just CALL the function and pass parameters.

Same as how you don't write HTTP — you call HttpClient.
Same as how you don't write JSON parsing — you call JsonSerializer.
OpenCV is just a library for image operations.
```

---

## Complexity Level

```
De-noise    →  1 line  →  cv2.medianBlur(img, 3)
De-skew     →  3 lines →  find angle + rotate
Binarize    →  1 line  →  cv2.adaptiveThreshold(...)

Total preprocessing code: ~15-20 lines
Skill needed: know WHICH function to call and WHAT parameters mean
              (not implement the math yourself)
```

---

## Interview Answer

> "Pre-processing is about 15-20 lines of OpenCV SDK calls — medianBlur for de-noise, minAreaRect + rotate for de-skew, adaptiveThreshold for binarization. OpenCV provides the algorithms; you just call the functions with the right parameters. The only judgment call is tuning the parameters for your specific document type — we found kernel size 3 for the median filter and a 5-degree skew tolerance worked for JM Family dealer forms."

## Claude

## De-Noise — Remove Dirt That Isn't Part of the Document

**Problem:** When you scan or fax a document, the machine introduces random dots that were never on the original paper.

```
BEFORE de-noise:
┌─────────────────────────────┐
│  P.a.t i•e n t:  J.o h n   │  ← random dots between letters
│  .D O B:  0 1/1•5/1 9 8 5  │  ← dots making characters unreadable
│  M•e d:  m e t•f o r m i n │
└─────────────────────────────┘

AFTER de-noise:
┌─────────────────────────────┐
│  Patient:  John             │  ← clean text
│  DOB:  01/15/1985           │
│  Med:  metformin            │
└─────────────────────────────┘
```

**How median filter works (simple explanation):**
```
Look at each pixel and its 8 neighbours.
Sort all 9 values. Take the middle (median) value.

A random noise dot is usually very different from its neighbours:
  0  0  0
  0 255  0   ← this white dot is noise (surrounded by black text pixels)
  0  0  0

Median of [0,0,0,0,255,0,0,0,0] = 0
→ noise dot replaced by 0 (black) → dot disappears

Real text pixels look similar to their neighbours → median keeps them unchanged
```

---

## De-Skew — Straighten a Tilted Document

**Problem:** Humans never place paper perfectly straight in a scanner. Even a few degrees of tilt causes OCR to misread characters.

```
BEFORE de-skew:
   P a t i e n t :  J o h n
      D O B :  0 1 / 1 5
         M e d :  m e t f o r m i n
            (document tilted 5 degrees)

OCR reads tilted text and gets confused —
characters that should be on the same line
appear at different heights to the model.

AFTER de-skew:
Patient:  John
DOB:  01/15/1985
Med:  metformin
(straight — OCR reads perfectly)
```

**How it works (simple explanation):**
```
Step 1: Find the dominant lines in the image
        Text lines create a pattern of dark pixels in rows
        The algorithm detects the ANGLE of those lines
        (this is what "Hough transform" does — finds lines)

Step 2: Calculate how many degrees the document is tilted
        "Lines are at 5 degrees from horizontal"

Step 3: Rotate the entire image by -5 degrees to correct it

Result: perfectly horizontal text → OCR accuracy jumps
JM Family: confidence went from 0.78 → 0.91 just from this step
```

---

## Binarize — Convert to Pure Black and White

**Problem:** Real scanned documents have grey backgrounds, shadows, uneven lighting — especially in corners. OCR models expect crisp black text on pure white background.

```
BEFORE binarize:
┌─────────────────────────────┐
│░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░│  ← grey background (not white)
│░░ Patient: John ░░░░░░░░░░░│  ← text on grey, dark corner bottom-right
│░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░│
│░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓│  ← dark shadow in corner
└─────────────────────────────┘

AFTER binarize:
┌─────────────────────────────┐
│                             │  ← pure white background
│  Patient: John              │  ← pure black text
│                             │
│                             │  ← shadow gone
└─────────────────────────────┘
```

**Why ADAPTIVE threshold (not simple threshold):**
```
Simple threshold: "if pixel > 128 → white, if < 128 → black"
Problem: the dark corner has pixel values around 80-100
         Simple threshold turns the whole corner black
         OCR sees a black blob instead of text

Adaptive threshold: instead of one global rule for the whole image,
                    it divides the image into small regions
                    and calculates the right threshold per region

Top-left corner (bright):  threshold = 200
Bottom-right corner (dark): threshold = 90
                            
Each region gets its own rule → shadow in corner handled correctly
→ text everywhere is crisp black on white
```

---

## All Three Together

```
RAW SCAN (tilted, noisy, grey background)
         ↓
DE-NOISE   → remove random dots from fax/scanner
         ↓
DE-SKEW    → rotate to make text perfectly horizontal
         ↓
BINARIZE   → convert to pure black and white
         ↓
CLEAN IMAGE → send to Azure Document Intelligence
             → confidence 0.91 instead of 0.78
```

Each step fixes one specific type of degradation. You always do them in this order — de-noise first because skew detection works better on a clean image, binarize last because it needs straight, clean text to threshold correctly.

---

## Claude

Two separate questions — let me answer both clearly.

---

## Question 1 — Where is confidence score measured?

**Code. Azure Document Intelligence returns it in the API response.**

```
YOU send:   cleaned image → Azure DI API

DI returns: {
  "fields": {
    "PatientName": {
      "content": "John Smith",
      "confidence": 0.94    ← DI calculated this
    },
    "DateOfBirth": {
      "content": "01/15/1985",
      "confidence": 0.71    ← DI calculated this
    },
    "Medication": {
      "content": "metf0rmin",   ← OCR misread
      "confidence": 0.68    ← DI calculated this (knows it's uncertain)
    }
  }
}
```

**You write code to READ that confidence and route:**
```python
for field_name, field in result.fields.items():
    score = field.confidence

    if score > 0.90:
        auto_process(field)        # high confidence → proceed
    elif score > 0.70:
        human_review_queue(field)  # uncertain → flag for human
    else:
        dead_letter(field)         # too low → reject
```

**You do NOT configure confidence thresholds in any portal.**
DI calculates the score internally. You decide what to DO with the score in your code.

---

## Question 2 — What are those parameters and how do you know them?

Let me go through each one:

**`cv2.medianBlur(img, 3)` — the `3`**
```
3 = kernel size (how many pixels to look at around each pixel)

Means: look at a 3×3 grid (9 pixels total), take the median

Why 3 and not 5 or 7?
├── 3 = removes small noise dots, preserves thin characters
├── 5 = removes bigger noise, but starts blurring thin text
└── 7 = too aggressive, text strokes get damaged

Rule: start with 3. If noise is heavy → try 5.
      Must be ODD number (3, 5, 7) — even numbers not allowed by OpenCV.
```

**`cv2.adaptiveThreshold(rotated, 255, cv2.ADAPTIVE_THRESH_GAUSSIAN_C, cv2.THRESH_BINARY, 11, 2)`**
```
Parameter 1: rotated      → your input image
Parameter 2: 255          → max value to assign (pure white = 255, always 255)
Parameter 3: ADAPTIVE_THRESH_GAUSSIAN_C → HOW to calculate local threshold
                            (Gaussian = weighted average of neighbourhood)
Parameter 4: THRESH_BINARY → output is pure black (0) or white (255), nothing in between
Parameter 5: 11           → block size — how large a region to analyse per local threshold
                            Must be ODD. 11 = looks at 11×11 pixel blocks
Parameter 6: 2            → constant subtracted from the calculated threshold
                            Fine-tunes sensitivity. 2 is a commonly used starting value.
```

---

## How Do You KNOW What Values to Use?

You don't guess — you **test and tune**:

```
Step 1: Take 20 sample documents from YOUR specific use case
        (JM Family dealer forms, VitalCare prior auth forms, etc.)

Step 2: Run with default/standard values first:
        medianBlur: 3
        blockSize: 11
        constant: 2

Step 3: Send pre-processed image to Azure DI
        Check the confidence scores that come back

Step 4: If confidence is low → adjust one parameter at a time:
        ├── Noise still visible? → increase blur kernel to 5
        ├── Text getting blurry? → reduce blur kernel to 1 (skip de-noise)
        ├── Dark corners still showing? → reduce block size to 7
        └── Too much text disappearing? → increase constant from 2 to 5

Step 5: Repeat until confidence scores are consistently above 0.90
```

**JM Family real example:**
```
Started with:  medianBlur(3), blockSize=11, constant=2
DI confidence: 0.78 (too low)

Tested de-skew: added Hough + rotate
DI confidence jumped to 0.91 ✅

→ The noise + binarize parameters were fine
→ The skew was the root cause
→ Found by testing, not by guessing
```

---

## One-Line Summary

> Confidence score = **Azure DI calculates it, you read it in code and route on it.**
> Parameters = **standard starting values from OpenCV docs, then tune by testing on your actual documents until DI confidence crosses your threshold.**

---

## You

are these constant values for all the documents are it varies the 3,5,7  in denoise and binrary 11,2 ?

---

## Claude

They **vary by document type** — but NOT per individual document at runtime. You tune them ONCE per document category, then fix them for that category in production.

---

## The Reality

```
NOT like this (changing per document at runtime):
Document 1 arrives → calculate parameters → use 3
Document 2 arrives → calculate parameters → use 5
Document 3 arrives → calculate parameters → use 3

LIKE THIS (fixed per document type, tuned once):
Prior Auth Forms    → always use medianBlur(3), blockSize=11
Faxed Lab Results   → always use medianBlur(5), blockSize=7
Insurance Cards     → skip de-noise, blockSize=15
```

---

## What Makes Parameters Different Per Document Type?

```
FACTOR 1 — How was it captured?
├── High-quality office scanner  → minimal noise → kernel 3 (or skip)
├── Standard scanner             → light noise   → kernel 3
├── Fax machine                  → heavy noise   → kernel 5
└── Phone camera photo           → very heavy    → kernel 7

FACTOR 2 — Font size / text density?
├── Large print (insurance card) → blockSize 15 (bigger regions)
└── Small print (lab results)    → blockSize 7  (smaller regions)

FACTOR 3 — Background type?
├── Pure white paper    → constant 2 (standard)
├── Coloured paper      → constant 5 (more aggressive)
└── Watermarked paper   → constant 7 (filter watermark)

FACTOR 4 — Document age?
├── Fresh print  → no degradation → kernel 3
└── 10-year-old  → yellowed, specks → kernel 5
```

---

## How You Discover the Right Values

```
One-time tuning process per document type:

1. Collect 50 sample documents of that TYPE
   (50 prior auth forms, 50 fax lab results, etc.)

2. Try standard values → run through DI → check confidence

3. Adjust ONE parameter at a time:

   Round 1: medianBlur = 3 → avg confidence 0.78 → too low
   Round 2: medianBlur = 5 → avg confidence 0.82 → better
   Round 3: medianBlur = 5, blockSize = 7 → avg confidence 0.91 ✅

4. Lock those values in config for that document type

5. Done — never change again unless document quality changes
```

---

## In Production Code — Per Document TYPE, Not Per Document

```python
# You define profiles per document type — tuned once, fixed forever
DOCUMENT_PROFILES = {
    "prior_auth_form": {
        "denoise_kernel": 3,      # clean office scans
        "block_size": 11,
        "constant": 2
    },
    "fax_lab_result": {
        "denoise_kernel": 5,      # heavy fax noise
        "block_size": 7,          # smaller text
        "constant": 3
    },
    "insurance_card": {
        "denoise_kernel": 1,      # phone photo, skip de-noise
        "block_size": 15,         # large print
        "constant": 2
    }
}

def preprocess(image, doc_type):
    profile = DOCUMENT_PROFILES[doc_type]
    denoised = cv2.medianBlur(image, profile["denoise_kernel"])
    binary = cv2.adaptiveThreshold(
        denoised, 255,
        cv2.ADAPTIVE_THRESH_GAUSSIAN_C,
        cv2.THRESH_BINARY,
        profile["block_size"],   # ← from profile
        profile["constant"]      # ← from profile
    )
    return binary
```

---

## Summary

| Question | Answer |
|---|---|
| Same values for all documents? | No — vary by document type |
| Change per individual document at runtime? | No — fixed per document category |
| How do you find the right values? | Test on 50 samples, tune until DI confidence ≥ 0.90 |
| How often do you retune? | Only if document quality changes (new scanner, new vendor) |
| Where are the values stored? | Config file / appsettings.json — not hardcoded |

> **One-liner:** Parameters are not universal constants and not runtime-calculated — they are **per-document-type settings you discover through one-time testing and then lock in configuration.**

## Stage 2 — Azure Document Intelligence

**Split: Portal Config for setup, SDK code for calling it**

```
PART A — Training the model (PORTAL ONLY, no code):
└── Open Document Intelligence Studio (portal)
└── Upload 10-15 sample forms
└── Draw boxes around fields (click and drag)
└── Label each box ("PatientName", "DateOfBirth", "Medication")
└── Click Train
└── Done — no code written

PART B — Calling the model (CODE, but very simple):
```

```csharp
// This is ALL the code needed to call Azure DI
var client = new DocumentAnalysisClient(endpoint, credential);

var operation = await client.StartAnalyzeDocumentAsync(
    "your-custom-model-id",   // from portal after training
    documentStream
);

var result = await operation.WaitForCompletionAsync();

// Read the results
foreach (var field in result.Documents[0].Fields)
{
    Console.WriteLine($"{field.Key}: {field.Value.Content} 
                       (confidence: {field.Value.Confidence})");
}
```

```
Total lines of code: ~10 lines
Complexity: LOW — just an API call + read the response
The heavy work (reading the form) happens INSIDE Azure DI
You just call it and read what comes back
```

---

## Stage 3 — Post-Processing

**100% code, no portal involved. But again — simple code.**

```csharp
// Format validation — is the date actually a valid date?
if (!DateTime.TryParse(fields["AdmissionDate"].Content, out _))
    await humanReviewQueue.SendAsync(documentId, "Invalid date format");

// Cross-field consistency — does discharge come after admission?
if (dischargeDate < admissionDate)
    await humanReviewQueue.SendAsync(documentId, "Discharge before admission");

// Business rules — does policy number match expected format?
if (!Regex.IsMatch(fields["PolicyNumber"].Content, @"^BCB-\d{6}$"))
    await humanReviewQueue.SendAsync(documentId, "Policy number format mismatch");

// Confidence routing
if (field.Confidence > 0.90)
    autoProcess(field);
else if (field.Confidence > 0.70)
    await humanReviewQueue.SendAsync(documentId, field.Key);
else
    await deadLetterQueue.SendAsync(documentId);
```

```
Total lines of code: ~30-40 lines
Complexity: LOW — basic if/else, DateTime.TryParse, Regex
No AI involved — just data validation rules you already know
```

---

## Full Picture

```
STAGE 1 — Pre-processing
├── Portal config:  Nothing
└── Code:           ~15 lines (OpenCV/ImageSharp SDK calls)

STAGE 2 — Azure DI
├── Portal config:  Label + train model (clicks, no code)
└── Code:           ~10 lines (API call + read response)

STAGE 3 — Post-processing
├── Portal config:  Nothing
└── Code:           ~30-40 lines (if/else validation rules)

TOTAL CODE: ~55-65 lines
TOTAL COMPLEXITY: LOW — SDK calls + basic validation
Nothing here requires advanced programming skills
```

---

## One-Liner Answer

> Stage 2 needs ~10 lines of code to call the API — the actual document reading is done by Azure DI, not by you. Stage 3 needs ~30-40 lines of basic validation rules — DateTime checks, Regex, if/else. Neither stage requires complex algorithms. The only portal-only step in the entire pipeline is labeling and training the DI model.

Im studying the module 06 agent workflow for the flow you given give me one simple example to cover the flow in it ?

## Claude

Simple example: **Nurse asks — "Is patient P-001 allergic to penicillin?"**

One question. One agent. Walk through every step of the module.

---

## Step 1 — Task Arrives

```
Nurse types into the clinical portal:
"Is patient P-001 allergic to penicillin?"

This becomes:
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ System message: "You are a clinical         │
│ assistant. You have 1 tool: EHR lookup.     │
│ Never guess. Only answer from retrieved     │
│ data."                                      │
│                                             │
│ User message: "Is patient P-001 allergic    │
│ to penicillin?"                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Agent wakes up. Reads both. Starts reasoning.
```

---

## Step 2 — ReAct Loop

```
ITERATION 1:
┌─ Reason ────────────────────────────────────┐
│ "I need the patient's allergy list.         │
│  I have an EHR tool. I'll call it."        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
     ↓ Act
calls: get_patient_record("P-001")
     ↓ Observe
returns: {
  name: "John Smith",
  allergies: ["penicillin", "sulfa drugs"],
  medications: ["metformin", "lisinopril"]
}

ITERATION 2:
┌─ Reason ────────────────────────────────────┐
│ "I have the allergy list.                   │
│  Penicillin is listed. I can answer now.   │
│  No more tool calls needed."               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Agent prepares answer:
"Yes — patient P-001 has a documented 
 penicillin allergy in their EHR record."
```

---

## Step 3 — Groundedness Check

```
Before the answer reaches the nurse:

Claim: "patient P-001 has a penicillin allergy"
Source retrieved: allergies: ["penicillin", "sulfa drugs"]

Is the claim supported by retrieved data? ✅ YES
Groundedness score: 0.97

0.97 ≥ 0.90 threshold → PASS
→ Answer delivered to nurse
```

---

## Step 4 — What If EHR Is Down? (Failure Path)

```
ITERATION 1:
Reason: "I need the patient's allergy list. I'll call EHR."
Act:    calls get_patient_record("P-001")
        → EHR API times out

Polly RetryPolicy:
└── Retry 1: wait 2s → timeout again
└── Retry 2: wait 4s → timeout again
└── Retry 3: wait 8s → timeout again
→ CircuitBreaker opens

Agent reasons:
"EHR unavailable. I cannot answer without
 verified patient data. I must not guess."

Answer to nurse:
"Unable to verify allergy status — 
 EHR system currently unavailable. 
 Please check patient chart directly 
 before administering any medication."

→ Not a guess. Not a hallucination.
→ Safe, honest, actionable response.
```

---

## Step 5 — Audit Trail

```
FunctionInvocationFilter logs automatically:

2:14:03 PM — ClinicalAgent called get_patient_record(P-001)
             → returned: allergies confirmed
             → success in 187ms

2:14:03 PM — Groundedness check → score 0.97 → PASS

2:14:03 PM — Response delivered to nurse [NurseID: N-042]
             CorrelationId: req-2026-06-28-P001-allergy

If EHR was down:
2:14:15 PM — get_patient_record(P-001) → FAILED after 3 retries
2:14:15 PM — Circuit OPEN → escalation response sent
```

---

## Step 6 — Monitoring

```
App Insights captures:
├── Token count: 312 tokens used
├── Latency: 890ms end-to-end
├── Tool calls: 1 (EHR lookup)
├── Groundedness score: 0.97
└── Outcome: SUCCESS

If this query ran 1,000 times today:
Azure Monitor dashboard shows:
├── Avg groundedness: 0.94 ✅
├── Avg latency: 910ms ✅
├── Tool failure rate: 0.3% ✅
└── No drift alerts
```

---

## The Whole Flow in One Picture

```
Nurse: "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"
              ↓
    [AGENT reads system prompt + question]
              ↓
    ReAct: "I need EHR data" → calls EHR tool
              ↓
    EHR returns allergy list
              ↓
    ReAct: "I have my answer" → stops loop
              ↓
    Groundedness check: claim vs EHR data → 0.97 ✅
              ↓
    "Yes — penicillin allergy documented"
              ↓
    Audit log: who asked, what was called, what was returned
              ↓
    App Insights: tokens, latency, groundedness tracked
              ↓
    Nurse gets safe, verified, grounded answer
```

---

**That single question — "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?" — covered every step in Module 06:**
- Task arrives → ReAct loop → tool call → groundedness check → failure path (EHR down) → audit trail → monitoring.

## You

how llm knows to pick the EHR  tool , do we need to let it in the prompt ?

---

## Claude

Great question. You do NOT tell the LLM "use the EHR tool" in the prompt. The LLM figures it out by reading the **tool descriptions** you wrote in code.

---

## How It Works

```
When you register a plugin in Semantic Kernel:

[KernelFunction]
[Description("Gets a patient's medical record including allergies,
              medications and conditions. Call this when you need
              any patient health information.")]
public async Task<PatientRecord> GetPatientRecord(string patientId)

SK automatically sends this description to the LLM
on EVERY call — like a menu of available tools.
```

---

## What the LLM Actually Sees

```
The LLM receives this behind the scenes (SK builds it automatically):

"You have these tools available:

Tool 1: GetPatientRecord
Description: Gets a patient's medical record including allergies,
             medications and conditions. Call this when you need
             any patient health information.
Parameters:  patientId (string)

Tool 2: CheckPolicyRules
Description: Looks up payer policy rules for a specific drug.
             Call this when you need insurance coverage criteria.
Parameters:  drugName (string), insurerName (string)"

Then the user asks:
"Is patient P-001 allergic to penicillin?"

LLM reasons:
"User needs patient health information
 → Tool 1 description matches exactly
 → I will call GetPatientRecord"
```

---

## The [Description] Is Everything

```
BAD description → LLM picks wrong tool or no tool:
[Description("Gets data")]
→ LLM has no idea when to use this

GOOD description → LLM picks correctly every time:
[Description("Gets a patient's medical record including allergies,
              medications and conditions. Call this when you need
              any patient health information.")]
→ "allergy" in the question matches "allergies" in description
→ LLM picks this tool confidently
```

---

## Do You Need to Mention It in the System Prompt?

```
System prompt:                Tool descriptions:
──────────────                ─────────────────
"You are a clinical           [Description("Gets patient
 assistant. Never              allergies and medications.
 guess. Only answer            Call when patient health
 from retrieved data."         info is needed.")]

You do NOT write:
"When asked about allergies, use GetPatientRecord"

The [Description] handles the routing automatically.
System prompt handles BEHAVIOUR (never guess, be safe).
Tool descriptions handle ROUTING (which tool to call when).
```

---

## Simple Summary

```
You register tools with descriptions
         ↓
SK sends tool list + descriptions to LLM automatically
         ↓
Nurse asks: "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"
         ↓
LLM reads: "allergy" → matches GetPatientRecord description
         ↓
LLM calls GetPatientRecord("P-001")
         ↓
You never wrote "use EHR tool" anywhere in your prompt
```

> **One-liner:** The LLM reads the `[Description]` attribute on each tool and matches it against the user's question. Good descriptions = correct tool selection. You never hardcode which tool to call — the LLM decides by reading the descriptions you wrote.

---

## You

sk only routes here LLM decides all ?

---

## Claude

Both have a role. Simple split:

```
LLM decides WHAT to do
SK executes WHAT the LLM decided
```

---

## The Exact Split

```
QUESTION: "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"
                    ↓
┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              LLM DECIDES                │
│                                         │
│  "I need patient health info.           │
│   GetPatientRecord matches.             │
│   Call it with patientId = 'P-001'"    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘
                    ↓
        LLM outputs a tool call request:
        { tool: "GetPatientRecord",
          parameters: { patientId: "P-001" } }
                    ↓
┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              SK EXECUTES                │
│                                         │
│  Sees the tool call request             │
│  Finds GetPatientRecord method in code  │
│  Runs the C# method                     │
│  Calls your FHIR API                    │
│  Gets the result back                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘
                    ↓
        Result sent back to LLM:
        { allergies: ["penicillin"] }
                    ↓
┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│          LLM DECIDES AGAIN              │
│                                         │
│  "I have the answer now.                │
│   No more tools needed.                 │
│   I will respond to the nurse."         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘
                    ↓
        "Yes — P-001 has penicillin allergy"
```

---

## Roles in One Table

| Who | Does What |
|---|---|
| **LLM** | Reads question, picks tool, picks parameters, reads result, decides next step, writes final answer |
| **SK** | Sends tool list to LLM, intercepts tool call request, runs the C# method, returns result to LLM |

---

## Simple Analogy

```
LLM = Doctor
SK  = Nurse

Doctor says: "Get me this patient's allergy chart"
Nurse goes and gets it, brings it back
Doctor reads it, makes the decision

Nurse never decides what to fetch.
Doctor never fetches it himself.
Each does their own job.
```

> **One-liner:** LLM decides which tool, which parameters, and what to do with the result. SK is the executor — it receives the LLM's decision, runs the actual C# code, and hands the result back to the LLM. The LLM is the brain, SK is the hands.

## You

groundness result is sent by llm ? who it knows the score the llm internally its configured to check the groundness ?

---

## Claude

The LLM does NOT check its own groundedness. It has no idea if its answer is grounded or not. That's the whole problem — it confidently produces wrong answers.

**Groundedness is checked by a SEPARATE process entirely.**

---

## Who Actually Checks Groundedness

```
TWO OPTIONS depending on where you are in the stack:

OPTION 1 — Azure AI Foundry Guardrail (Portal Config)
OPTION 2 — Custom check in SK code (Code)
```

---

## Option 1 — Azure AI Foundry (Portal Config)

```
You enable groundedness detection in Foundry portal.
Foundry adds an automatic step AFTER the agent answers:

Agent LLM generates:
"Yes — P-001 has a penicillin allergy"
          ↓
FOUNDRY GROUNDEDNESS CHECKER runs automatically:
          ↓
A SECOND LLM call (GPT-4o as judge) receives:

  "Agent answer: Yes — P-001 has penicillin allergy
   Retrieved data: { allergies: ['penicillin', 'sulfa'] }

   Question: Is the agent's answer supported
             by the retrieved data? Score 0-1."
          ↓
Judge LLM replies: "Score: 0.97 — fully supported"
          ↓
Score ≥ 0.90 → Foundry releases answer to nurse
Score < 0.90 → Foundry BLOCKS answer, escalates
```

```
Key point:
Agent LLM  = answers the question (does not check itself)
Judge LLM  = separately checks if the answer is grounded
These are TWO separate LLM calls
```

---

## Option 2 — Custom Check in SK Code

```csharp
// After agent generates answer, YOU call the evaluation API
var groundednessScore = await _evaluationClient.EvaluateGroundednessAsync(
    answer: agentResponse,          // what the agent said
    context: retrievedChunks        // what was retrieved from EHR/RAG
);

if (groundednessScore >= 0.90)
    return agentResponse;           // safe to deliver
else
    await escalationQueue.SendAsync(sessionId, agentResponse);
```

---

## Why LLM Cannot Check Its Own Groundedness

```
This is the core problem from Module 03:

LLM has NO internal confidence signal.

A model that is 40% sure produces text that
SOUNDS exactly as confident as when it is 99% sure.

You cannot ask the agent LLM:
"Are you grounded?" → it will say "Yes" even when hallucinating

That is why you need a SEPARATE judge:
└── Give judge: agent answer + retrieved data
└── Judge compares them independently
└── Judge has no bias toward the original answer
└── Judge scores objectively
```

---

## Full Picture

```
Nurse question
      ↓
Agent LLM reasons + calls EHR tool    ← AGENT LLM
      ↓
Agent LLM generates answer             ← AGENT LLM
      ↓
STOP — answer held, not delivered yet
      ↓
Judge LLM receives:                    ← SEPARATE JUDGE LLM
  answer + retrieved EHR data
  "Is this answer supported?"
      ↓
Judge scores: 0.97
      ↓
Score ≥ 0.90 → release to nurse
Score < 0.90 → block, escalate
```

---

## Summary

| | Who does it | How configured |
|---|---|---|
| **Generate answer** | Agent LLM | Your system prompt + tools |
| **Check groundedness** | Separate Judge LLM | Portal toggle (Foundry) OR code (SK eval API) |
| **Route on score** | Your code | if/else on the score |

> **One-liner:** The agent LLM generates the answer and has no idea if it is grounded. A completely separate Judge LLM — triggered by Azure Foundry guardrail (Config) or your SK code — receives the answer + the retrieved data and scores whether the answer is supported. Two LLMs, two jobs, completely independent.

---

## You

my question was who gives the gourndness result ? if its llm ? how it does that ?

---

## Claude

The Judge is another LLM call. It gives the score by reading a specially crafted prompt that YOU (or Foundry) send it.

---

## How the Judge LLM Gets the Score

It is not magic. It is just another LLM call with a specific prompt:

```
Foundry sends this to GPT-4o (the judge):

"You are a groundedness evaluator.

Retrieved data:
'Patient P-001 allergies: penicillin, sulfa drugs'

Agent answer:
'Yes — patient P-001 has a penicillin allergy'

Question:
Is every claim in the agent answer directly 
supported by the retrieved data above?

Score from 0.0 to 1.0.
0.0 = nothing supported
1.0 = fully supported by retrieved data"
```

```
Judge LLM reads both texts, compares them, replies:

"The claim 'penicillin allergy' is directly 
 present in the retrieved data. 
 Score: 0.97"
```

---

## The Score Is Just Text Parsing

```
Judge LLM outputs:  "Score: 0.97"
                          ↓
Foundry/your code reads:  0.97
                          ↓
Compare against threshold: 0.97 ≥ 0.90 ✅
                          ↓
Release answer to nurse
```

---

## Simple Diagram

```
AGENT LLM                    JUDGE LLM
─────────                    ─────────
Receives question            Receives special prompt:
Calls EHR tool               ├── retrieved data
Gets allergy data            ├── agent answer  
Generates answer             └── "score this 0-1"
"P-001 has penicillin        
 allergy"                    Compares the two texts
      ↓                      Outputs: "Score: 0.97"
      └──────────────────────────────────────────┐
                                                 ↓
                                         Your code reads 0.97
                                         0.97 ≥ 0.90 → deliver
```

---

## Why Does the Judge LLM Know How to Score?

```
It doesn't have special groundedness knowledge.
It is just a very capable text comparison model.

The prompt tells it EXACTLY what to do:
"Compare these two texts.
 Does text B (answer) come from text A (retrieved data)?
 Give me a number."

GPT-4o is very good at this comparison task.
Microsoft trained/prompted it specifically for this role
inside Azure AI Foundry guardrails.
```

---

## One-Liner

> The Judge LLM is just another GPT-4o call. Foundry (or your code) sends it a prompt containing the retrieved data AND the agent answer and asks "is this answer supported by this data, score 0 to 1?" The Judge reads both texts, compares them, and outputs a number. Your code reads that number and decides to release or block the answer.

---

## You

i still dont understand the concept of groundnes. my question is simple who generates the groundness score first initial one ?

---

## Claude

The Judge LLM generates it. First time, every time.

---

```
EVERY single response goes through this:

Step 1: Agent LLM answers
        "P-001 has penicillin allergy"

Step 2: Judge LLM scores it
        Reads answer + retrieved data
        Outputs: 0.97

Step 3: Your code checks the score
        0.97 ≥ 0.90 → deliver to nurse
```

---

There is no "initial" vs "later" — it happens fresh every single response.

```
Request 1:  Agent answers → Judge scores → 0.97 → deliver
Request 2:  Agent answers → Judge scores → 0.88 → block
Request 3:  Agent answers → Judge scores → 0.94 → deliver
Request 4:  Agent answers → Judge scores → 0.71 → block
```

Every time. Same process. Judge LLM generates the score from scratch each time by comparing that response's answer against that response's retrieved data.

---

> **One sentence:** The Judge LLM generates the groundedness score — every single response, fresh, by comparing what the agent said against what was retrieved.

---

## You

is the judg LLM different model or sam as agent LLM ?

---

## Claude

Same model, different role.

---

```
AGENT CALL:
You send GPT-4o:
"You are a clinical assistant...
 Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"
→ GPT-4o answers the question

JUDGE CALL:
You send GPT-4o AGAIN:
"You are a groundedness evaluator...
 Score this answer against this data: 0-1"
→ GPT-4o scores the answer
```

---

Same GPT-4o model. Two separate API calls. Different prompts = different roles.

```
Think of it like ONE doctor doing TWO jobs:

Job 1: Doctor reads patient chart → gives diagnosis
       (Agent role)

Job 2: Same doctor reads diagnosis + chart again
       → confirms "yes my diagnosis matches the chart"
       (Judge role)

Same person. Two separate tasks. Different instructions each time.
```

---

## Can You Use a Different Model as Judge?

Yes — and sometimes you should:

```
Agent:  GPT-4o        (expensive, best reasoning)
Judge:  GPT-4o-mini   (cheaper, sufficient for comparison)

Cost saving: judge call is simpler task
             GPT-4o-mini can compare two texts accurately
             No need to spend full GPT-4o cost on scoring

In Azure AI Foundry: Foundry picks the judge model for you
In custom SK code:   you choose which model to call for judging
```

---

> **One-liner:** Same model by default — GPT-4o called twice with different prompts. First call answers the question, second call scores the answer. You can optionally use a cheaper model (GPT-4o-mini) for the judge call to save cost.

## You

how the judge llm validates the result again it verifies in the rag, cag ?

---

## Claude

The Judge does NOT go back to RAG or CAG. The retrieved chunks are passed directly to the Judge in the prompt.

---

```
RAG retrieves chunks ONCE:
Chunk: "Patient P-001 allergies: penicillin, sulfa drugs"
                    ↓
        chunks passed to AGENT LLM
        Agent answers: "P-001 has penicillin allergy"
                    ↓
        SAME chunks passed to JUDGE LLM
        Judge compares answer vs chunks
        No new retrieval. No going back to database.
```

---

## What Judge LLM Actually Receives

```
Judge receives ONE prompt containing BOTH:

"Retrieved chunk:
 'Patient P-001 allergies: penicillin, sulfa drugs'
                    ↑
           already retrieved, passed as text

 Agent answer:
 'P-001 has penicillin allergy'
                    ↑
           agent's response, passed as text

 Does the answer come from the chunk? Score 0-1"

Judge reads both texts IN THE PROMPT.
No database. No RAG call. No CAG lookup.
Pure text comparison inside one LLM call.
```

---

## Full Flow — Where RAG Happens Only Once

```
User question
      ↓
RAG retrieves chunks from vector DB ← ONLY TIME RAG IS CALLED
      ↓
Chunks stored in memory for this request
      ↓
      ├──→ Sent to AGENT LLM (to answer the question)
      │
      └──→ Sent to JUDGE LLM (to verify the answer)
      
Vector DB never called again.
Judge works entirely from what was already retrieved.
```

---

> **One-liner:** RAG retrieves chunks once. Those same chunks are passed to both the Agent LLM (to generate the answer) and the Judge LLM (to verify the answer). The Judge never goes back to the database — it compares the answer against the already-retrieved chunks that were handed to it in the prompt.

## You

so in the sesssion each thread of message is maintained and sent as whole message with all the thread message ? or chathistory is sent ineach prompt

---

## Claude

ChatHistory is sent in every prompt — the whole conversation so far, every time.

---

```
Turn 1:
You send to LLM:
  System: "You are a clinical assistant..."
  User:   "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"

LLM replies:
  Assistant: "Yes — P-001 has penicillin allergy"

─────────────────────────────────────────────

Turn 2:
You send to LLM:
  System:    "You are a clinical assistant..."
  User:      "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"    ← Turn 1 repeated
  Assistant: "Yes — P-001 has penicillin allergy"  ← Turn 1 repeated
  User:      "What medications is P-001 on?"       ← NEW question

─────────────────────────────────────────────

Turn 3:
You send to LLM:
  System:    "You are a clinical assistant..."
  User:      "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"    ← repeated
  Assistant: "Yes — P-001 has penicillin allergy"  ← repeated
  User:      "What medications is P-001 on?"       ← repeated
  Assistant: "P-001 is on metformin, lisinopril"   ← repeated
  User:      "Can P-001 take amoxicillin?"         ← NEW question
```

---

## Why Send Everything Every Time?

```
LLM is STATELESS.

After each response it forgets everything.
No memory between calls.
It is like a doctor with amnesia —
every time you walk in the door
they remember nothing about you.

ChatHistory is how you give them their memory back:
"Here is everything we discussed so far.
 Now answer this new question."
```

---

## Who Maintains ChatHistory?

```
NOT the LLM.
NOT Azure.

YOUR CODE maintains it.

In Semantic Kernel:
var history = new ChatHistory();

// Turn 1
history.AddUserMessage("Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?");
var reply = await kernel.InvokeAsync(..., history);
history.AddAssistantMessage(reply);    // ← you store it

// Turn 2
history.AddUserMessage("What medications is P-001 on?");
var reply2 = await kernel.InvokeAsync(..., history);
history.AddAssistantMessage(reply2);   // ← you store it

// history object grows with every turn
// SK sends the whole thing to LLM each time
```

---

## The Problem With This

```
Turn 1:   500 tokens sent
Turn 5:  2,000 tokens sent
Turn 20: 8,000 tokens sent
Turn 50: context window limit hit → error

Solution (from Module 06 token optimization):

Rolling window:  keep only last 10 turns, drop oldest
Summarization:   compress old turns into one summary message
                 "Previous context: P-001 allergy confirmed,
                  medications: metformin, lisinopril"
                 Replace 20 turns with 1 summary = 80% token reduction
```

---

> **One-liner:** The entire ChatHistory is sent to the LLM on every single call — LLM is stateless and remembers nothing between calls. Your SK code maintains the ChatHistory object, adds each turn to it, and sends the whole thing every time. As conversations grow longer, you apply rolling window or summarization to keep token count under control.

---

## You

In agent framework sk or azure foundry give me the high level of how the message request that is prompt message looks like for 3 chat in same session ?

---

## Claude

Here is exactly what gets sent to the LLM API for each of 3 chats in the same session:

---

## Chat 1 — First Message

```json
POST to Azure OpenAI API:

{
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "system",
      "content": "You are a clinical assistant. You have 
                  one tool: get_patient_record. Never guess."
    },
    {
      "role": "user",
      "content": "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"
    }
  ]
}
```
```
LLM replies → tool call request:
{ "tool": "get_patient_record", "patientId": "P-001" }

SK runs the tool → gets result → sends back to LLM → LLM answers:
"Yes — P-001 has a penicillin allergy"

SK stores everything in ChatHistory.
```

---

## Chat 2 — Second Message

```json
POST to Azure OpenAI API:

{
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "system",
      "content": "You are a clinical assistant. You have 
                  one tool: get_patient_record. Never guess."
    },
    {
      "role": "user",                              ← TURN 1 repeated
      "content": "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"
    },
    {
      "role": "assistant",                         ← TURN 1 repeated
      "content": "Yes — P-001 has a penicillin allergy"
    },
    {
      "role": "user",                              ← NEW
      "content": "What medications is P-001 on?"
    }
  ]
}
```
```
LLM already knows P-001's allergy from Turn 1 history.
LLM calls get_patient_record again for medications.
SK stores the new turn in ChatHistory.
```

---

## Chat 3 — Third Message

```json
POST to Azure OpenAI API:

{
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "system",
      "content": "You are a clinical assistant. You have 
                  one tool: get_patient_record. Never guess."
    },
    {
      "role": "user",                              ← TURN 1 repeated
      "content": "Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?"
    },
    {
      "role": "assistant",                         ← TURN 1 repeated
      "content": "Yes — P-001 has a penicillin allergy"
    },
    {
      "role": "user",                              ← TURN 2 repeated
      "content": "What medications is P-001 on?"
    },
    {
      "role": "assistant",                         ← TURN 2 repeated
      "content": "P-001 is on metformin and lisinopril"
    },
    {
      "role": "user",                              ← NEW
      "content": "Can P-001 take amoxicillin?"
    }
  ]
}
```

---

## The Pattern You Can See

```
Every chat adds 2 more entries to the messages array:
├── Previous user message    (role: user)
└── Previous assistant reply (role: assistant)

Chat 1 → messages array: 2 items  (system + user)
Chat 2 → messages array: 4 items  (+ user + assistant from Turn 1)
Chat 3 → messages array: 6 items  (+ user + assistant from Turn 2)
Chat 10→ messages array: 20 items (keeps growing)

System message is ALWAYS first, ALWAYS repeated.
New question is ALWAYS last.
Everything in between = history.
```

---

## Who Builds This Array?

```
YOU never build this JSON manually.

SK builds it automatically from ChatHistory:

var history = new ChatHistory();
history.AddSystemMessage("You are a clinical assistant...");

// Turn 1
history.AddUserMessage("Is P-001 allergic to penicillin?");
// SK sends history → gets reply
history.AddAssistantMessage("Yes — P-001 has penicillin allergy");

// Turn 2
history.AddUserMessage("What medications is P-001 on?");
// SK sends history → gets reply
history.AddAssistantMessage("P-001 is on metformin, lisinopril");

// Turn 3
history.AddUserMessage("Can P-001 take amoxicillin?");
// SK converts ChatHistory → messages array → sends to API
```

---

> **One-liner:** Every API call sends the complete messages array — system prompt first, then every previous user + assistant turn, then the new question last. The array grows by 2 entries per chat turn. SK builds this array automatically from your ChatHistory object — you never write the JSON yourself.
